Public Agencies: National/state forest timber programs Private Sector: Industrial timber management, consulting forestry NGOs: Working forest conservation, sustainable forestry certification Consulting: Timber management planning, appraisal, procurement Academia: Sustainable forestry research
1.4 WILDLIFE & FISHERIES BIOLOGY
Understanding animal populations and their management
Skills related to understanding wildlife and fish species, their habitats, and sustainable management approaches.
________________


1.4.1 Population dynamics and modeling Understanding how animal populations grow, decline, and respond to changes in their environment over time.
Job Tasks:
1. Analyze population trends using field data and statistical models
2. Simulate population responses to habitat changes or management actions
3. Provide population viability assessments for at-risk species
Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:
FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years) Knowledge: Understands basic population concepts (birth/death rates, carrying capacity, density dependence) Application: Collects population data and assists with basic analyses Indicators:
* Explains population growth models (exponential, logistic)
* Collects mark-recapture or count data following protocols
* Calculates basic population metrics (density, sex ratio, age structure)
* Understands factors affecting population change
* Plots population trends over time
* Recognizes population cycles and patterns
INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years) Knowledge: Applies population ecology theory and statistical methods Application: Analyzes population data and makes management recommendations Indicators:
* Conducts mark-recapture analysis (Lincoln-Petersen, Jolly-Seber)
* Uses survival analysis and life table methods
* Analyzes population trends using statistical software (Program MARK, R)
* Estimates vital rates (survival, fecundity, recruitment)
* Identifies density-dependent and independent factors
* Uses harvest models to set sustainable take levels
* Incorporates uncertainty into population estimates
* Presents population status to managers and stakeholders
ADVANCED (6-10 years) Knowledge: Expert in population modeling; understands complex population dynamics Application: Develops population models for management and conservation Indicators:
* Builds population viability models (PVA) for threatened species
* Uses matrix models and individual-based models
* Incorporates environmental stochasticity and demographic uncertainty
* Models metapopulation dynamics and connectivity
* Predicts population responses to climate change
* Evaluates effectiveness of management actions on populations
* Develops adaptive harvest management frameworks
* Publishes population ecology research
* Trains wildlife professionals in population analysis
EXPERT (10+ years) Knowledge: Shapes population ecology science and application Application: Leads major population research or management programs Indicators:
* Develops innovative population modeling approaches
* Influences population management policy and standards
* Leads endangered species recovery programs
* Publishes influential population ecology research
* Provides expert testimony on population issues
Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Wildlife population management, endangered species recovery, harvest management Private Sector: Wildlife population monitoring on private lands, hunting operations NGOs: Species conservation, population monitoring programs Consulting: Population assessments for development projects, conservation planning Academia: Population ecology research, conservation biology
________________


1.4.2 Wildlife survey techniques Methods for counting and monitoring different animal species to track population health and distribution.
Job Tasks:
1. Deploy trail cameras, acoustic sensors, or live traps to monitor wildlife presence
2. Conduct aerial or ground-based surveys to estimate species abundance
3. Train field crews in standardized wildlife monitoring protocols
Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:
FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years) Knowledge: Understands basic survey methods for different taxa Application: Conducts wildlife surveys following established protocols Indicators:
* Conducts point counts, transects, or plot surveys
* Sets trail cameras and downloads/processes images
* Uses binoculars and spotting scopes for visual surveys
* Records observations on standardized datasheets
* Follows animal handling protocols safely and humanely
* Recognizes survey biases and detection probability issues
* Uses GPS to record survey locations accurately
INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years) Knowledge: Applies survey design principles; understands detectability and sampling theory Application: Designs and implements survey programs Indicators:
* Designs statistically valid survey protocols
* Selects appropriate survey methods for species and objectives
* Uses distance sampling methods and analysis
* Conducts occupancy surveys and modeling
* Deploys and manages acoustic monitoring arrays
* Uses mark-recapture techniques
* Analyzes detection probability and accounts for imperfect detection
* Trains and supervises field crews
* Uses specialized equipment (radio telemetry, thermal imaging)
ADVANCED (6-10 years) Knowledge: Expert in wildlife survey methodology Application: Leads complex multi-species monitoring programs Indicators:
* Develops comprehensive monitoring programs across landscapes
* Uses advanced techniques (eDNA, drones, automated sensors)
* Integrates multiple survey methods for robust inference
* Evaluates survey effectiveness and cost-efficiency
* Uses citizen science and community-based monitoring
* Applies AI/machine learning for automated species detection
* Publishes on survey methodology
* Trains wildlife professionals in survey techniques
* Develops regional or national monitoring protocols
EXPERT (10+ years) Knowledge: Shapes wildlife monitoring science and standards Application: Leads transformative monitoring initiatives Indicators:
* Develops innovative monitoring approaches adopted widely
* Influences national/international monitoring standards
* Leads large-scale monitoring programs (e.g., national bird surveys)
* Publishes influential survey methodology research
* Provides expert guidance on monitoring design
Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Wildlife population surveys, species inventory, monitoring programs Private Sector: Wildlife monitoring for mitigation, game management surveys NGOs: Species monitoring, citizen science programs Consulting: Baseline wildlife surveys, pre/post-project monitoring Academia: Survey methodology research, long-term studies
________________


1.4.3 Species identification and classification Accurately identifying different animals and understanding how they are related taxonomically.
Job Tasks:
1. Use field guides, keys, or genetic tools to identify species accurately
2. Record and classify species observations in biological databases
3. Train staff or volunteers on species recognition and data recording
Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:
FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years) Knowledge: Knows common species in local area; understands basic taxonomy Application: Identifies common species in the field Indicators:
* Identifies common local wildlife to species level
* Uses field guides and identification keys
* Distinguishes similar species using field marks
* Records species observations with accurate nomenclature
* Understands taxonomic hierarchy (kingdom to species)
* Recognizes when expert verification is needed
* Photographs specimens for documentation
INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years) Knowledge: Expert in regional fauna; understands functional morphology and taxonomy Application: Identifies diverse species including difficult taxa Indicators:
* Identifies most regional species including challenging groups
* Uses morphological keys for difficult identifications
* Identifies species from signs (tracks, scat, calls, nests)
* Verifies identifications using multiple characteristics
* Understands subspecies and geographic variation
* Uses bioacoustics for species identification
* Maintains specimen collections for reference
* Trains others in species identification
* Contributes to species checklists and databases
ADVANCED (6-10 years) Knowledge: Taxonomic expert for specific groups; understands evolutionary relationships Application: Serves as identification authority; resolves difficult identifications Indicators:
* Expert identifier for challenging taxonomic groups
* Uses genetic tools for identification (DNA barcoding, eDNA)
* Identifies cryptic species and subspecies
* Understands phylogenetic relationships and nomenclatural changes
* Discovers or documents range extensions or new species records
* Curates museum collections or voucher specimens
* Publishes taxonomic papers or field guides
* Provides identification training and workshops
* Reviews records for rare species committees
EXPERT (10+ years) Knowledge: Shapes taxonomic understanding of groups Application: Contributes to taxonomic revisions; influences classification Indicators:
* Describes new species or revises taxonomy
* Publishes taxonomic monographs or revisions
* Serves on taxonomic committees (AOU, ASM, etc.)
* Influences changes in scientific nomenclature
* Maintains authoritative reference collections
* Recognized international expert for taxa
Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Species documentation, rare species verification, inventory programs Private Sector: Species lists for projects, wildlife surveys NGOs: Biodiversity documentation, rare species monitoring Consulting: Species identification for environmental assessments Academia: Taxonomy research, museum curation, systematic biology
________________


1.4.4 Animal behavior and habitat relationships Understanding how animals behave in their environment and what habitat features they need to survive.
Job Tasks:
1. Study movement patterns, mating behavior, or foraging strategies in the field
2. Correlate wildlife use patterns with habitat variables using GPS or GIS
3. Recommend habitat improvements based on behavioral observations
Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:
FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years) Knowledge: Understands basic animal behavior concepts and habitat needs Application: Observes and records animal behavior Indicators:
* Describes basic behaviors (feeding, nesting, territorial, social)
* Records behavioral observations systematically
* Identifies habitat features used by wildlife (cover, food, water)
* Recognizes seasonal behavior patterns (migration, breeding, hibernation)
* Understands predator-prey interactions
* Observes wildlife without causing disturbance
INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years) Knowledge: Applies animal behavior theory; understands resource selection Application: Analyzes habitat use and behavior patterns Indicators:
* Conducts focal animal sampling and behavioral studies
* Uses GPS telemetry to study animal movements
* Analyzes habitat selection using GIS and resource selection functions
* Identifies limiting resources and habitat bottlenecks
* Evaluates habitat quality based on behavioral indicators
* Designs habitat improvements based on behavioral needs
* Studies foraging ecology and diet composition
* Assesses human impacts on animal behavior
ADVANCED (6-10 years) Knowledge: Expert in behavioral ecology and habitat relationships Application: Leads research on animal behavior and habitat use Indicators:
* Designs comprehensive behavioral ecology studies
* Uses advanced telemetry (GPS, accelerometers, satellite tags)
* Models habitat selection at multiple spatial scales
* Studies behavioral responses to environmental change
* Evaluates habitat connectivity and movement corridors
* Integrates behavior into conservation strategies
* Uses behavioral indicators for population assessment
* Publishes behavioral ecology research
* Trains wildlife professionals in behavioral studies
EXPERT (10+ years) Knowledge: Shapes understanding of animal behavior and conservation Application: Leads transformative behavioral ecology programs Indicators:
* Develops innovative approaches to studying behavior
* Influences how behavior informs conservation practice
* Leads major behavioral ecology research programs
* Publishes influential behavioral ecology research
* Integrates behavior, evolution, and conservation
Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Habitat management, human-wildlife conflict resolution, movement ecology Private Sector: Wildlife-friendly infrastructure design, behavioral mitigation NGOs: Wildlife corridor conservation, habitat protection Consulting: Behavioral impact assessments, habitat mitigation design Academia: Behavioral ecology research, animal cognition
________________


1.4.5 Fisheries management principles Approaches to sustainably managing fish populations in lakes, rivers, and streams for conservation or recreation.
Job Tasks:
1. Assess fish populations using netting, electrofishing, or sonar
2. Recommend harvest limits, stocking plans, or habitat improvements for fishery sustainability
3. Monitor water quality and aquatic habitat conditions affecting fish health
Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:
FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years) Knowledge: Understands basic fisheries concepts and survey methods Application: Assists with fish sampling and data collection Indicators:
* Conducts electrofishing, gill netting, or seine sampling
* Identifies common fish species
* Measures fish (length, weight) and collects scales/otoliths for aging
* Records catch data accurately
* Understands basic fish biology and life cycles
* Recognizes signs of poor fish health or habitat degradation
* Follows fish handling protocols to minimize stress
INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years) Knowledge: Applies fisheries science; understands population dynamics and harvest Application: Conducts fisheries assessments and develops recommendations Indicators:
* Designs fish population surveys
* Analyzes catch data to estimate abundance and size structure
* Conducts age and growth analysis
* Uses catch curves and mortality estimates
* Develops harvest recommendations (catch limits, slot limits, seasons)
* Evaluates stocking programs
* Assesses fish habitat quality and limiting factors
* Integrates water quality with fisheries management
* Uses fish community indices for ecosystem assessment
ADVANCED (6-10 years) Knowledge: Expert in fisheries science and aquatic ecosystem management Application: Leads comprehensive fisheries management programs Indicators:
* Develops sustainable fisheries management plans
* Uses stock assessment models (surplus production, age-structured)
* Implements adaptive management for fisheries
* Manages complex multi-species fisheries
* Evaluates and restores fish habitat
* Designs fish passage and connectivity improvements
* Addresses invasive species impacts on fisheries
* Incorporates climate change into fisheries management
* Publishes fisheries research
* Trains fisheries professionals
EXPERT (10+ years) Knowledge: Shapes fisheries science and management practice Application: Leads major fisheries programs or policy initiatives Indicators:
* Influences fisheries policy and regulations
* Develops innovative fisheries management approaches
* Leads ecosystem-based fisheries management initiatives
* Publishes influential fisheries research
* Provides expert consultation on complex fisheries issues
* Advances sustainable fisheries at regional/national level
Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: State/federal fisheries programs, endangered fish recovery Private Sector: Aquaculture, private pond management, fishing guide services NGOs: Fisheries conservation, river restoration, angler engagement Consulting: Fisheries impact assessments, mitigation design Academia: Fisheries science research, aquatic ecology


1.5 CLIMATE SCIENCE
Understanding how climate systems affect ecosystems and how to plan for climate change impacts

Skills related to interpreting climate data, assessing vulnerability to climate change, and developing science-based adaptation strategies. These skills enable land managers to anticipate and respond to changing conditions rather than managing for historical baselines that may no longer be achievable.

________________


1.5.1 Climate data analysis and interpretation
Understanding and using climate data products—from historical records to future projections—to inform land management decisions.

Job Tasks:
1. Download, process, and interpret downscaled climate projections for a management unit
2. Analyze historical climate trends to identify shifts in temperature, precipitation, or extreme events
3. Translate climate model outputs into actionable information for resource managers and planners

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands basic climate concepts (weather vs. climate, climate variability, greenhouse effect)
Application: Accesses and interprets basic climate data products
Indicators:
* Explains difference between weather and climate
* Understands climate normals and how they're calculated
* Accesses data from Climate Data Online, PRISM, or similar sources
* Reads and interprets climate graphs and maps
* Understands basic concepts of climate models and projections
* Recognizes limitations of climate data (scale, uncertainty)

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies climate science to ecological and management questions; understands projection methods
Application: Analyzes climate data and communicates findings to managers
Indicators:
* Uses downscaled climate projections (CMIP, LOCA, MACA)
* Selects appropriate climate variables for ecological applications
* Calculates climate metrics relevant to ecosystems (growing degree days, drought indices, fire weather)
* Interprets ensemble projections and communicates uncertainty
* Compares historical trends to projected changes
* Uses climate data in GIS for spatial analysis
* Explains emissions scenarios (RCPs, SSPs) and their implications
* Integrates climate data into planning documents

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in climate data sources, methods, and limitations; understands regional climate dynamics
Application: Leads climate data analysis for complex planning efforts
Indicators:
* Evaluates and selects among climate data products for specific applications
* Conducts bias correction and statistical downscaling
* Develops climate summaries for agency planning (forest plans, resource management plans)
* Analyzes extreme event probabilities and return intervals
* Integrates multiple climate variables into ecological assessments
* Communicates climate information effectively to diverse audiences
* Trains staff in climate data interpretation
* Publishes climate analysis for management applications

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes how climate science informs natural resource management
Application: Provides climate science leadership at regional or national scales
Indicators:
* Develops climate data standards and protocols for agencies
* Leads development of climate adaptation strategies
* Influences how climate projections are used in policy and planning
* Collaborates with climate modeling community on applied needs
* Publishes influential work on climate-ecology connections
* Provides expert consultation to senior leadership on climate science

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Forest plan climate analysis, climate screening for infrastructure, drought planning
Private Sector: Climate risk assessment for assets, climate services consulting, insurance applications
NGOs: Climate vulnerability assessments for conservation priorities, climate communication
Consulting: Climate analysis for NEPA, climate risk disclosure support
Academia: Climate impacts research, climate data product development

________________


1.5.2 Climate vulnerability assessment
Systematically evaluating how climate change threatens species, ecosystems, infrastructure, and communities to prioritize adaptation actions.

Job Tasks:
1. Conduct vulnerability assessments for focal species or ecosystems using established frameworks
2. Identify climate exposure, sensitivity, and adaptive capacity for management units
3. Synthesize vulnerability findings into actionable recommendations for managers

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands vulnerability framework concepts (exposure, sensitivity, adaptive capacity)
Application: Assists with vulnerability assessments using established protocols
Indicators:
* Explains the three components of vulnerability
* Gathers information on species or ecosystem climate sensitivities
* Assists with expert elicitation or scoring exercises
* Documents vulnerability assessment findings
* Understands how vulnerability informs adaptation planning
* Recognizes different vulnerability assessment approaches

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies vulnerability assessment frameworks; understands ecological climate sensitivities
Application: Conducts vulnerability assessments for species, ecosystems, or areas
Indicators:
* Leads vulnerability assessments using standard frameworks (e.g., NatureServe CCVI, System for Assessing Vulnerability of Species)
* Identifies appropriate spatial and temporal scales for assessment
* Synthesizes literature on climate sensitivities
* Facilitates expert input on sensitivity and adaptive capacity
* Integrates exposure projections with sensitivity analysis
* Prioritizes species or areas by vulnerability
* Communicates vulnerability findings to managers
* Connects vulnerability to management options

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in vulnerability science; understands multiple frameworks and their applications
Application: Designs and leads comprehensive vulnerability assessments
Indicators:
* Designs vulnerability assessments for complex multi-resource situations
* Adapts frameworks to specific contexts and management needs
* Conducts landscape-scale vulnerability assessments
* Integrates socioeconomic and ecological vulnerability
* Addresses uncertainty in vulnerability ratings
* Develops agency vulnerability assessment protocols
* Trains staff in vulnerability assessment methods
* Publishes vulnerability assessment research

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Advances vulnerability assessment science and application
Application: Shapes how vulnerability assessment informs conservation and management
Indicators:
* Develops new vulnerability assessment frameworks or methods
* Influences national or international vulnerability assessment standards
* Leads multi-agency vulnerability initiatives
* Publishes influential vulnerability research
* Advises senior leadership on vulnerability priorities

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: National forest vulnerability assessments, wildlife action plan climate chapters, infrastructure risk assessment
Private Sector: Asset vulnerability assessment, supply chain climate risk, natural capital risk
NGOs: Conservation priority vulnerability ranking, climate-smart conservation planning
Consulting: Vulnerability assessments for NEPA, climate risk disclosure
Academia: Vulnerability framework development, comparative vulnerability research

________________


1.5.3 Climate adaptation planning
Developing strategies and actions to help species, ecosystems, and human communities adjust to changing climate conditions.

Job Tasks:
1. Facilitate adaptation planning workshops with resource managers and stakeholders
2. Develop climate adaptation strategies that align with agency mandates and planning frameworks
3. Identify and evaluate adaptation options using decision-support tools

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands adaptation concepts (resistance, resilience, transformation); knows adaptation differs from mitigation
Application: Supports adaptation planning processes
Indicators:
* Explains adaptation strategies (resist, accept, direct)
* Distinguishes adaptation from mitigation
* Assists with adaptation workshops and documentation
* Identifies examples of adaptation actions
* Understands barriers to adaptation implementation
* Recognizes maladaptation risks

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies adaptation frameworks; understands how adaptation integrates with existing planning
Application: Develops adaptation strategies and facilitates planning processes
Indicators:
* Facilitates adaptation planning using established frameworks (e.g., Adaptation Workbook, Climate-Smart Conservation)
* Connects vulnerability findings to adaptation options
* Evaluates adaptation options against multiple criteria
* Integrates adaptation into existing plans (forest plans, wildlife plans)
* Identifies co-benefits and tradeoffs among options
* Addresses implementation barriers
* Monitors adaptation effectiveness
* Communicates adaptation strategies to diverse audiences

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in adaptation science and planning; understands transformation and managed change
Application: Leads complex adaptation initiatives across programs or landscapes
Indicators:
* Designs adaptation planning processes for multi-resource, multi-stakeholder situations
* Addresses transformation when resistance and resilience are insufficient
* Integrates adaptation across planning scales (project to landscape to regional)
* Develops agency adaptation guidance and protocols
* Evaluates adaptation effectiveness and promotes adaptive management
* Addresses social and institutional barriers to adaptation
* Trains staff in adaptation planning
* Publishes adaptation planning research

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes adaptation science and practice at field or policy levels
Application: Provides adaptation leadership nationally or internationally
Indicators:
* Develops innovative adaptation frameworks or approaches
* Influences national adaptation policy and guidance
* Leads transformative adaptation initiatives
* Publishes influential adaptation research
* Advises senior leadership on adaptation strategy
* Advances equity and justice in adaptation planning

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Forest plan adaptation components, climate-informed wildlife management, infrastructure adaptation
Private Sector: Corporate climate adaptation strategy, climate-resilient supply chains
NGOs: Climate-smart conservation implementation, adaptation advocacy
Consulting: Adaptation planning support, climate resilience assessment
Academia: Adaptation science research, adaptation planning frameworks

________________


1.5.4 Carbon cycle science
Understanding how carbon moves through ecosystems and how land management affects carbon storage and emissions.

Job Tasks:
1. Estimate carbon stocks and fluxes in forests, soils, or other ecosystems using field measurements and models
2. Evaluate how management alternatives affect carbon sequestration and storage
3. Support carbon accounting for agency reporting, project development, or carbon markets

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands basic carbon cycle concepts (pools, fluxes, sequestration)
Application: Collects carbon-related field data; uses basic carbon tools
Indicators:
* Explains major carbon pools in ecosystems (biomass, soil, dead wood)
* Understands photosynthesis and respiration as carbon fluxes
* Collects field data for carbon estimation (tree measurements, soil samples)
* Uses basic carbon calculators or lookup tables
* Understands relationship between carbon and climate
* Recognizes how disturbance affects carbon

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies carbon accounting methods; understands carbon dynamics in managed systems
Application: Estimates carbon stocks and evaluates management effects on carbon
Indicators:
* Uses allometric equations and models to estimate biomass carbon
* Conducts forest carbon inventories using standard protocols
* Estimates soil carbon using appropriate methods
* Models carbon dynamics under different management scenarios
* Understands carbon accounting frameworks (IPCC guidelines, forest inventory)
* Evaluates tradeoffs between carbon and other objectives
* Communicates carbon information to managers
* Supports carbon-related planning and reporting

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in carbon science; understands carbon across ecosystems and management contexts
Application: Leads carbon assessment programs and integrates carbon into planning
Indicators:
* Designs carbon monitoring programs
* Uses process-based models for carbon dynamics
* Integrates carbon with forest planning and NEPA
* Evaluates carbon market project eligibility and additionality
* Addresses uncertainty in carbon estimates
* Develops agency carbon assessment protocols
* Analyzes landscape carbon dynamics and legacy effects
* Publishes carbon science research
* Trains staff in carbon methods

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes carbon science and its application to land management
Application: Provides carbon science leadership at regional or national scales
Indicators:
* Develops carbon accounting standards or methods
* Influences carbon policy and reporting requirements
* Leads major carbon assessment or monitoring initiatives
* Publishes influential carbon research
* Advises leadership on carbon management strategy
* Advances integration of carbon into multi-objective management

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: National forest carbon assessments, agency carbon reporting, carbon-informed planning
Private Sector: Forest carbon project development, corporate carbon footprint, carbon offset verification
NGOs: Nature-based solutions advocacy, carbon project development
Consulting: Carbon inventory services, carbon market support, lifecycle assessment
Academia: Carbon cycle research, carbon modeling, methodology development

________________


1.5.5 Phenology and climate-biosphere interactions
Understanding how seasonal biological events (flowering, migration, emergence) respond to climate and how these changes cascade through ecosystems.

Job Tasks:
1. Monitor phenological events (bud break, flowering, migration timing) using field observation or remote sensing
2. Analyze phenological trends and their relationships to climate variables
3. Assess phenological mismatches and their ecological consequences

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands phenology concepts and why timing matters ecologically
Application: Collects phenological observations using standard protocols
Indicators:
* Explains what phenology is and why it matters
* Recognizes major phenological events in local ecosystems
* Collects phenological data using established protocols (e.g., Nature's Notebook)
* Understands how temperature and photoperiod trigger phenological events
* Recognizes concept of phenological mismatch
* Uses phenological calendars or databases

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies phenological science; understands climate-phenology relationships
Application: Analyzes phenological data and interprets trends
Indicators:
* Analyzes phenological time series for trends
* Relates phenological shifts to climate drivers
* Uses remote sensing indices (NDVI, EVI) for landscape phenology
* Identifies potential phenological mismatches
* Integrates phenology into species or ecosystem assessments
* Designs phenological monitoring protocols
* Communicates phenological changes to managers
* Uses phenology models to project future timing

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in phenological science and its ecological implications
Application: Leads phenological research and applies findings to management
Indicators:
* Designs comprehensive phenological monitoring programs
* Models phenological responses to climate change
* Assesses consequences of phenological shifts for species interactions
* Integrates phenology into vulnerability assessments
* Uses phenology to inform management timing (prescribed fire, restoration planting)
* Publishes phenological research
* Trains practitioners in phenological monitoring
* Develops phenology-informed management recommendations

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes phenological science and its application to conservation
Application: Provides phenology leadership and innovation
Indicators:
* Develops novel phenological monitoring or modeling approaches
* Influences national phenological networks or standards
* Leads major phenology research initiatives
* Publishes influential phenological research
* Advises on phenology implications for policy and management

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Phenological monitoring networks, management timing guidance, climate indicator tracking
Private Sector: Agricultural phenology services, pest emergence prediction
NGOs: Citizen science phenology programs, climate change communication
Consulting: Phenological assessment for impact analysis
Academia: Phenology research, climate-ecology interactions

1.6 BIOTECHNOLOGY & GENETICS
Using molecular and genetic tools to understand and conserve biodiversity

Skills related to applying genetic and genomic technologies to conservation and management challenges. These skills enable practitioners to detect species, assess genetic diversity, inform breeding and translocation decisions, and understand evolutionary responses to environmental change. As molecular tools become more accessible, these skills increasingly bridge laboratory science and field management.

________________


1.6.1 Conservation genetics principles
Understanding how genetic diversity, gene flow, and evolutionary processes affect population viability and conservation outcomes.

Job Tasks:
1. Interpret genetic study results to inform translocation, reintroduction, or management unit decisions
2. Assess genetic diversity and structure in populations of conservation concern
3. Advise managers on genetic considerations in species recovery planning

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands basic genetic concepts (alleles, genetic diversity, gene flow, genetic drift)
Application: Recognizes when genetic considerations are relevant to management
Indicators:
* Explains why genetic diversity matters for population viability
* Understands concepts of inbreeding and inbreeding depression
* Recognizes factors that reduce genetic diversity (small population size, isolation)
* Identifies situations where genetic analysis might inform management
* Reads and summarizes genetic study findings
* Understands difference between genetics and genomics

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies conservation genetics concepts; interprets genetic data and study designs
Application: Integrates genetic information into management recommendations
Indicators:
* Interprets population genetic metrics (heterozygosity, FST, effective population size)
* Understands genetic management unit delineation
* Evaluates genetic study designs for management applications
* Applies genetic guidelines to translocation and reintroduction planning
* Recognizes genetic rescue as a management tool
* Assesses connectivity from a genetic perspective
* Communicates genetic findings to managers
* Identifies when specialist genetic consultation is needed

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in conservation genetics; understands landscape genetics and evolutionary potential
Application: Leads integration of genetics into conservation programs
Indicators:
* Designs genetic monitoring programs
* Applies landscape genetics to connectivity planning
* Evaluates adaptive genetic variation and evolutionary potential
* Develops genetic management guidelines for species or programs
* Integrates genetics into recovery plans and habitat conservation plans
* Addresses genetic considerations in assisted migration decisions
* Collaborates with geneticists on applied research
* Publishes conservation genetics applications
* Trains staff in genetic concepts

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes conservation genetics practice and policy
Application: Provides genetics leadership nationally or internationally
Indicators:
* Develops conservation genetics guidelines or policies
* Influences how genetics is integrated into endangered species management
* Leads major conservation genetics initiatives
* Publishes influential conservation genetics research
* Advises leadership on genetic management strategies
* Advances integration of genetics into routine conservation practice

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: ESA recovery planning genetics, genetic management of small populations, genetic monitoring
Private Sector: Genetic assessment for mitigation, conservation breeding programs
NGOs: Genetic considerations in conservation prioritization, genetic rescue advocacy
Consulting: Genetic surveys and analysis, genetic management recommendations
Academia: Conservation genetics research, method development

________________


1.6.2 Environmental DNA (eDNA) methods
Using DNA shed by organisms into their environment (water, soil, air) to detect species presence without direct observation.

Job Tasks:
1. Design and implement eDNA sampling protocols for species detection
2. Interpret eDNA results and communicate detection findings with appropriate uncertainty
3. Evaluate when eDNA is appropriate versus traditional survey methods

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands eDNA concepts (DNA shedding, persistence, detection)
Application: Collects eDNA samples following established protocols
Indicators:
* Explains how eDNA detection works
* Understands contamination risks and prevention
* Collects water or soil samples for eDNA using proper technique
* Follows chain of custody and sample handling protocols
* Recognizes limitations of eDNA (detection vs. abundance, false positives/negatives)
* Understands difference between single-species and metabarcoding approaches

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies eDNA methods; understands study design and interpretation
Application: Designs eDNA surveys and interprets results for management
Indicators:
* Designs eDNA sampling strategies (site selection, timing, replication)
* Selects appropriate eDNA methods for objectives (qPCR vs. metabarcoding)
* Interprets detection probabilities and occupancy from eDNA data
* Evaluates eDNA results alongside traditional survey data
* Communicates eDNA findings with appropriate caveats
* Understands primer selection and assay validation
* Identifies quality laboratories for eDNA analysis
* Applies eDNA to invasive species early detection

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in eDNA science; understands method development and limitations
Application: Leads eDNA programs and advances applications
Indicators:
* Develops eDNA monitoring programs for agencies or regions
* Addresses eDNA challenges (inhibition, degradation, imperfect detection)
* Integrates eDNA with other monitoring approaches
* Evaluates new eDNA technologies and applications
* Develops eDNA protocols and standards
* Uses eDNA for biodiversity assessment (metabarcoding community analysis)
* Publishes eDNA methodology or applications
* Trains staff in eDNA methods
* Collaborates with labs on assay development

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes eDNA science and application in conservation
Application: Provides eDNA leadership and innovation
Indicators:
* Develops novel eDNA applications or methods
* Influences eDNA standards and best practices
* Leads major eDNA research or monitoring initiatives
* Publishes influential eDNA research
* Advises on eDNA policy and regulatory applications

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Rare species surveys, invasive species early detection, ESA compliance monitoring
Private Sector: Pre-project species surveys, aquatic monitoring, environmental compliance
NGOs: Biodiversity assessment, conservation monitoring, citizen science eDNA
Consulting: eDNA surveys for permitting, species detection services
Academia: eDNA method development, ecology research applications

________________


1.6.3 Population genomics and genetic diversity
Using genome-wide data to assess genetic health, adaptation, and evolutionary history of populations.

Job Tasks:
1. Interpret genomic analyses of population structure, diversity, and adaptive variation
2. Apply genomic findings to inform management of genetic diversity
3. Evaluate inbreeding, genetic load, and evolutionary potential in populations of concern

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)

Application: Recognizes when genomic approaches offer advantages over traditional genetics
Indicators:
* Explains difference between genetics (few markers) and genomics (genome-wide)
* Understands concepts of SNPs, whole genome sequencing, RAD-seq
* Recognizes that genomics can reveal adaptive variation
* Identifies situations where genomic data might improve management
* Reads and summarizes genomic study findings

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies population genomics concepts; interprets common analyses
Application: Uses genomic information in conservation planning
Indicators:
* Interprets population structure from genomic data
* Understands measures of genomic diversity and inbreeding
* Recognizes signatures of selection and local adaptation
* Applies genomic findings to source population selection
* Evaluates studies of adaptive genetic variation
* Communicates genomic findings to managers
* Understands data management needs for genomic datasets
* Identifies appropriate genomic approaches for management questions

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in population genomics applications; understands analytical approaches
Application: Leads integration of genomics into conservation programs
Indicators:
* Designs genomic studies for management applications
* Interprets landscape genomics and genotype-environment associations
* Applies genomics to predict climate adaptation potential
* Uses genomics to inform genetic rescue decisions
* Integrates genomics into species recovery planning
* Collaborates with genomicists on applied research
* Publishes applied genomics research
* Develops guidelines for genomic applications
* Trains staff in genomic concepts

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes how genomics informs conservation practice
Application: Provides genomics leadership nationally or internationally
Indicators:
* Develops novel genomic applications for conservation
* Influences policy on genomic approaches in species management
* Leads major conservation genomics initiatives
* Publishes influential genomics research
* Advises on genomic management strategies

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Genomic assessment of listed species, adaptive potential evaluation, genetic rescue planning
Private Sector: Genomic services, breeding program optimization
NGOs: Genomic prioritization of conservation investments
Consulting: Genomic analysis services, conservation breeding support
Academia: Population genomics research, method development

________________

Applying genetic and genomic information directly to management decisions including translocations, captive breeding, and genetic rescue.

3. Evaluate and implement genetic rescue for inbred populations

* Recognizes genetic rescue as a conservation tool

Indicators:
* Applies genetic guidelines to translocation planning
* Evaluates source population options based on genetic data
* Understands pedigree management in captive breeding
* Assesses when genetic rescue might benefit a population
* Communicates genetic management recommendations
* Monitors genetic outcomes of management actions
* Balances genetic considerations with other factors (behavior, disease, logistics)

ADVANCED (6-10 years)

Application: Leads genetic management programs
Indicators:
* Develops genetic management plans for species or populations
* Designs genetic monitoring for translocation programs

* Develops agency genetic management protocols

Indicators:
* Develops national genetic management guidelines
* Influences policy on genetic intervention in conservation
* Leads major genetic management initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises leadership on genetic management strategies
* Addresses emerging genetic technologies (gene editing, synthetic biology)

1.7 NOVEL ECOSYSTEMS & ADAPTATION
Managing ecosystems transformed beyond historical conditions by climate change, invasive species, and other persistent drivers

Skills related to understanding and managing ecosystems that have fundamentally changed from their historical state. As climate change and other pressures push systems beyond restoration to historical conditions, these skills help practitioners recognize transformation, set appropriate goals, and manage for function and value rather than historical fidelity. This emerging area bridges ecological science with policy frameworks that may not yet accommodate novel conditions.

________________


1.7.1 Novel ecosystem recognition and assessment
Identifying when ecosystems have crossed thresholds into fundamentally new states and assessing their ecological function and conservation value.

Job Tasks:
1. Evaluate whether an ecosystem has transformed beyond its historical range of variability
2. Assess ecological function and conservation value in ecosystems with novel species compositions
3. Determine when restoration to historical conditions is feasible versus when transformation is irreversible

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands novel ecosystem concepts and why they're increasingly relevant
Application: Recognizes examples of novel ecosystems
Indicators:
* Explains what makes an ecosystem "novel" versus degraded
* Understands concepts of ecological thresholds and state changes
* Recognizes common drivers of ecosystem transformation (climate, invasives, altered disturbance)
* Identifies examples of novel ecosystems locally or regionally
* Understands why historical baselines may be unachievable
* Recognizes controversy around novel ecosystem management

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies novel ecosystem concepts; understands assessment approaches
Application: Assesses ecosystem novelty and evaluates function
Indicators:
* Evaluates degree of compositional and functional novelty
* Assesses whether transformation appears reversible
* Identifies what ecological functions persist in novel systems
* Evaluates conservation value (supporting native species, providing services)
* Documents novel ecosystem characteristics
* Communicates novel ecosystem concepts to managers and stakeholders
* Distinguishes productive novel systems from degraded systems
* Integrates novel ecosystem thinking into assessments

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in novel ecosystem science; understands management implications
Application: Leads assessment and planning for novel ecosystems
Indicators:
* Develops frameworks for assessing novel ecosystem function and value
* Evaluates ecological services in transformed systems
* Addresses policy and legal implications of accepting novelty
* Integrates novel ecosystem concepts into planning documents
* Develops guidance for managing in context of transformation
* Publishes novel ecosystem research
* Trains staff in novel ecosystem concepts
* Navigates stakeholder and institutional resistance to novelty frameworks

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes novel ecosystem science and management practice
Application: Provides thought leadership on ecosystem transformation
Indicators:
* Develops conceptual frameworks for novel ecosystems
* Influences policy accommodation of ecosystem transformation
* Leads initiatives addressing novel ecosystems
* Publishes influential research
* Advises leadership on managing transformation

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Forest plan desired conditions under transformation, wildlife habitat assessment in novel systems
Private Sector: Ecosystem service valuation in novel systems, adaptive management frameworks
NGOs: Conservation strategy in transforming landscapes, stakeholder communication
Consulting: Novel ecosystem assessment, restoration feasibility analysis
Academia: Novel ecosystem research, conceptual development

________________


1.7.2 Assisted migration and managed relocation
Intentionally moving species to new locations to help them track suitable climate or escape threats they cannot avoid naturally.

Job Tasks:
1. Evaluate candidate species and situations for assisted migration
2. Assess risks and benefits of proposed managed relocations
3. Develop protocols for assisted migration implementation and monitoring

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands assisted migration concepts and the debate surrounding it
Application: Recognizes when assisted migration might be considered
Indicators:
* Explains why species may not naturally track climate change
* Understands difference between assisted migration, managed relocation, and introduction
* Recognizes arguments for and against assisted migration
* Identifies barriers to natural dispersal (habitat fragmentation, dispersal limitation)
* Understands regulatory considerations (introduction of non-native species)
* Recognizes that assisted migration remains controversial

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies assisted migration decision frameworks; understands risk assessment
Application: Evaluates assisted migration proposals
Indicators:
* Uses decision frameworks to evaluate assisted migration candidates
* Assesses climate exposure and dispersal limitation
* Evaluates invasion risk and potential impacts on recipient ecosystems
* Reviews source population genetics and adaptation
* Assesses feasibility including regulatory pathways
* Documents assisted migration rationale and monitoring plans
* Communicates assisted migration decisions to stakeholders
* Distinguishes assisted migration from routine translocations

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in assisted migration science and implementation
Application: Leads assisted migration programs and policy development
Indicators:
* Designs assisted migration programs including monitoring
* Develops agency assisted migration guidance
* Addresses policy and regulatory barriers
* Integrates assisted migration with broader adaptation strategies
* Evaluates assisted migration outcomes and refines approaches
* Publishes assisted migration research
* Trains staff in assisted migration concepts
* Navigates stakeholder and scientific controversy

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes assisted migration practice and policy
Application: Provides national leadership on assisted migration
Indicators:
* Develops national assisted migration policies or guidelines
* Influences regulatory treatment of climate-motivated translocations
* Leads major assisted migration initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises leadership on assisted migration strategy

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Forestry assisted migration (seed zones), wildlife managed relocation, policy development
Private Sector: Climate-adapted seed sources, assisted migration for mitigation
NGOs: Assisted migration advocacy or opposition, conservation translocations
Consulting: Assisted migration feasibility assessment, risk evaluation
Academia: Assisted migration research, policy analysis

________________


1.7.3 Climate refugia identification
Locating areas where species may persist as surrounding habitats become unsuitable, and prioritizing these areas for protection.

Job Tasks:
1. Map potential climate refugia using topographic, hydrologic, and climate data
2. Evaluate refugia characteristics and their capacity to support climate-sensitive species
3. Integrate refugia identification into conservation prioritization and land protection

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands refugia concepts and why they matter for conservation
Application: Recognizes potential refugia features
Indicators:
* Explains what climate refugia are and how they function
* Understands different refugia types (topographic, hydrologic, edaphic)
* Recognizes landscape features that create microclimatic buffering
* Understands why refugia are conservation priorities
* Identifies potential refugia in local landscapes

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies refugia identification methods; understands modeling approaches
Application: Maps and evaluates potential refugia
Indicators:
* Uses GIS to model topographic and hydrologic refugia
* Applies climate velocity and exposure mapping
* Identifies cold-air pooling, north-facing slopes, groundwater-dependent features
* Evaluates refugia size, connectivity, and habitat quality
* Integrates refugia into conservation prioritization
* Assesses species-specific refugia requirements
* Communicates refugia concepts to managers
* Documents refugia characteristics and conservation value

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in refugia science; understands temporal dynamics and management
Application: Leads refugia identification and conservation programs
Indicators:
* Develops regional refugia identification frameworks
* Models refugia persistence under different climate scenarios
* Integrates refugia into land acquisition and protection priorities
* Addresses management of refugia (reducing non-climate stressors)
* Evaluates refugia connectivity and stepping stones
* Publishes refugia research
* Trains staff in refugia concepts
* Develops refugia conservation strategies

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes refugia science and conservation application
Application: Provides refugia leadership regionally or nationally
Indicators:
* Develops novel refugia identification approaches
* Influences refugia conservation policy and priorities
* Leads major refugia conservation initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises on refugia in conservation strategy

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Land acquisition prioritization, protected area management, climate adaptation planning
Private Sector: Conservation development, mitigation site selection
NGOs: Land protection priorities, conservation easement targeting
Consulting: Refugia analysis for conservation planning
Academia: Refugia research, modeling approaches

________________

Managing ecosystems undergoing fundamental change toward new states, balancing intervention with acceptance of transformation.

Job Tasks:
1. Develop management strategies for ecosystems in transition
2. Set appropriate goals when historical conditions are unachievable
3. Manage to sustain ecological function and value during transformation

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands that some ecosystem change is irreversible and management must adapt
Application: Recognizes transformation and its management implications
Indicators:
* Explains why some ecosystems cannot be restored to historical conditions
* Understands concepts of ecological thresholds and tipping points
* Recognizes when management goals may need adjustment
* Identifies ongoing transformation in local ecosystems
* Understands emotional and institutional challenges of accepting change

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies transformation concepts; understands adaptive goal-setting
Application: Develops management approaches for transforming systems
Indicators:
* Assesses trajectory of transforming ecosystems
* Develops management goals focused on function rather than composition
* Identifies management actions that support beneficial transformation
* Recognizes when intervention is futile versus productive
* Communicates transformation concepts to stakeholders
* Documents rationale for adaptive goal-setting
* Monitors transformation outcomes
* Integrates transformation into planning frameworks

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in transformation ecology and management
Application: Leads management of transforming landscapes
Indicators:
* Develops transformation management frameworks
* Sets landscape-scale priorities during transformation
* Addresses policy constraints on adaptive management
* Integrates transformation into planning documents (forest plans, wildlife plans)
* Navigates institutional and legal barriers to accepting transformation
* Publishes transformation management research
* Trains staff in transformation concepts
* Develops stakeholder engagement approaches for transformation

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes transformation management practice and policy
Application: Provides transformation leadership
Indicators:
* Develops policies accommodating ecological transformation
* Influences legal and regulatory frameworks to enable adaptive management
* Leads transformative initiatives addressing ecosystem change
* Publishes influential research
* Advises leadership on transformation management

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Forest plan adaptation, wildlife management in changing systems, fire management adaptation
Private Sector: Adaptive asset management, transformation risk assessment
NGOs: Conservation strategy under transformation, stakeholder communication
Consulting: Transformation assessment, adaptive planning support
Academia: Transformation ecology research, policy analysis

1.7.5 Non-analog futures planning
Planning for ecological conditions with no historical precedent, where past patterns provide limited guidance for management.

Job Tasks:
1. Identify when future conditions are likely to be non-analog (no-analog climates, novel species combinations)
2. Develop planning approaches robust to unprecedented conditions
3. Build flexibility and adaptive capacity into long-term plans

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands that future conditions may have no historical precedent
Application: Recognizes non-analog concepts
Indicators:
* Explains what non-analog climates and ecosystems mean
* Understands why historical reference conditions have limitations
* Recognizes that species may form new combinations
* Identifies where non-analog conditions are projected locally
* Understands implications for management planning

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies non-analog concepts to planning; understands scenario approaches
Application: Integrates non-analog thinking into planning
Indicators:
* Identifies where non-analog conditions are likely
* Uses scenario planning to address uncertainty
* Develops robust strategies across multiple futures
* Builds flexibility into management plans
* Communicates non-analog concepts to stakeholders
* Avoids over-reliance on historical patterns
* Documents rationale for non-analog planning approaches

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in planning under deep uncertainty
Application: Leads planning for non-analog futures
Indicators:
* Develops planning frameworks for non-analog conditions
* Uses structured decision-making under uncertainty
* Builds adaptive capacity into plans and institutions
* Addresses policy frameworks tied to historical baselines
* Publishes non-analog planning research
* Trains staff in uncertainty planning
* Develops decision triggers and monitoring for adaptation

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes planning practice for unprecedented futures
Application: Provides leadership on planning under uncertainty
Indicators:
* Develops novel planning approaches for non-analog futures
* Influences policy to accommodate uncertainty
* Leads initiatives addressing unprecedented change
* Publishes influential research
* Advises leadership on planning under deep uncertainty

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: Long-term planning (forest plans, resource management plans), infrastructure planning
Private Sector: Long-term asset planning, scenario analysis
NGOs: Conservation strategy under uncertainty, climate communication
Consulting: Scenario planning services, uncertainty analysis
Academia: Decision science research, planning methodology

1.8 SUSTAINABLE AGRICULTURE SCIENCE
Understanding agricultural systems that maintain productivity while enhancing ecosystem services and environmental quality

Skills related to the scientific principles underlying agricultural systems that work with ecological processes rather than against them. These skills bridge agricultural science with ecological science, supporting management of working lands for multiple outcomes including production, carbon sequestration, water quality, biodiversity, and climate resilience.

________________


1.8.1 Agroecology principles
Understanding the ecological principles that govern agricultural systems and how to apply them for sustainable production.

Job Tasks:
1. Analyze agricultural systems using ecological frameworks (energy flow, nutrient cycling, species interactions)
2. Evaluate how agricultural practices affect ecosystem processes
3. Design or recommend farming approaches based on ecological principles

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands that agriculture is embedded in ecological systems
Application: Recognizes agroecological principles in practice
Indicators:
* Explains how ecological concepts apply to agriculture
* Understands nutrient cycling in agricultural systems
* Recognizes beneficial species interactions (pollinators, natural enemies, soil organisms)
* Identifies ecological impacts of conventional practices
* Understands concept of ecosystem services from agriculture
* Recognizes examples of agroecological practices

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies agroecological principles to analyze and improve agricultural systems
Application: Evaluates and recommends agroecological approaches
Indicators:
* Analyzes energy and nutrient flows in farming systems
* Evaluates biodiversity in agricultural landscapes (field to landscape scale)
* Assesses ecological functions on farms (pollination, pest regulation, water regulation)
* Recommends practices that enhance ecological function
* Evaluates tradeoffs between production and ecosystem services
* Communicates agroecological principles to farmers and managers
* Integrates agroecology into conservation planning on working lands

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in agroecological science; understands system design and transition
Application: Leads agroecological assessment and transition programs
Indicators:
* Designs agroecological systems for specific contexts
* Evaluates agroecological transitions at farm and landscape scales
* Assesses ecosystem service provision from agricultural landscapes
* Develops agroecological assessment frameworks
* Publishes agroecology research
* Trains staff and producers in agroecological concepts
* Integrates agroecology into policy and program design

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes agroecological science and application
Application: Provides agroecology leadership
Indicators:
* Develops innovative agroecological frameworks
* Influences agricultural policy toward agroecological approaches
* Leads major agroecology initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises on agroecological transformation

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: NRCS conservation planning, USFS grazing management, working lands programs
Private Sector: Sustainable sourcing, agricultural consulting, certification
NGOs: Sustainable agriculture advocacy, farmer technical assistance
Consulting: Agroecological assessment, farm planning
Academia: Agroecology research, farming systems analysis

________________


1.8.2 Soil carbon dynamics
Understanding how carbon accumulates and is lost from agricultural soils, and how management affects soil carbon storage.

Job Tasks:
1. Measure or estimate soil carbon stocks and changes in agricultural systems
2. Evaluate how management practices affect soil carbon accumulation
3. Support soil carbon programs including carbon markets and climate-smart agriculture

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands that soils store carbon and management affects storage
Application: Collects soil samples and supports carbon assessment
Indicators:
* Explains why soil carbon matters (climate, soil health)
* Understands soil organic matter components
* Collects soil samples using proper protocols
* Recognizes practices that build or deplete soil carbon
* Understands basic soil carbon measurement approaches
* Recognizes challenges in measuring soil carbon change

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies soil carbon science; understands measurement and management
Application: Assesses soil carbon and evaluates management effects
Indicators:
* Designs soil carbon sampling protocols
* Uses soil carbon models (COMET-Farm, RothC, Century)
* Evaluates management practice effects on soil carbon
* Understands soil carbon saturation and persistence
* Assesses eligibility and additionality for carbon programs
* Communicates soil carbon concepts to farmers and managers
* Recognizes limitations of soil carbon measurement and modeling
* Integrates soil carbon into conservation planning

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in soil carbon science; understands dynamics across systems and scales
Application: Leads soil carbon programs and advances applications
Indicators:
* Develops soil carbon monitoring programs
* Evaluates soil carbon MRV (measurement, reporting, verification) approaches
* Addresses uncertainty in soil carbon quantification
* Integrates soil carbon with other soil health indicators
* Develops soil carbon program guidance
* Publishes soil carbon research
* Trains staff and producers in soil carbon concepts
* Evaluates soil carbon market opportunities

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes soil carbon science and policy application
Application: Provides soil carbon leadership
Indicators:
* Develops soil carbon accounting standards
* Influences soil carbon policy and programs
* Leads major soil carbon initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises on soil carbon strategy

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: NRCS soil health programs, climate-smart agriculture, soil carbon reporting
Private Sector: Carbon market project development, corporate supply chain, soil carbon verification
NGOs: Soil carbon program development, farmer technical assistance
Consulting: Soil carbon assessment, carbon market support
Academia: Soil carbon research, method development

________________


1.8.3 Regenerative agriculture systems
Understanding farming approaches that restore soil health, sequester carbon, enhance biodiversity, and improve watershed function.

Job Tasks:
1. Evaluate regenerative practices and their outcomes across multiple dimensions
2. Support farmers transitioning to regenerative approaches
3. Assess and document regenerative agriculture outcomes for programs or markets

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands regenerative agriculture concepts and practices
Application: Recognizes regenerative practices and their intended outcomes
Indicators:
* Explains regenerative agriculture principles (minimize disturbance, keep soil covered, maintain living roots, diversify)
* Identifies common regenerative practices (no-till, cover crops, diverse rotations, integrated livestock)
* Understands intended outcomes (soil health, carbon, water, biodiversity)
* Recognizes that "regenerative" lacks standardized definition
* Identifies examples of regenerative systems

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies regenerative concepts; understands practice interactions and context dependence
Application: Evaluates and supports regenerative systems
Indicators:
* Assesses regenerative practice suitability for specific contexts
* Understands how practices interact as systems
* Evaluates multiple outcomes (soil health, carbon, water, biodiversity, economics)
* Supports farmers in regenerative transition
* Recognizes barriers to adoption (equipment, knowledge, risk, economics)
* Communicates regenerative concepts accurately
* Documents regenerative outcomes
* Distinguishes substantive regenerative approaches from greenwashing

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in regenerative systems; understands evidence base and limitations
Application: Leads regenerative agriculture programs
Indicators:
* Designs regenerative agriculture assessment frameworks
* Evaluates evidence for regenerative outcomes
* Develops program guidance for regenerative practices
* Addresses measurement and verification challenges
* Integrates regenerative agriculture into conservation programs
* Publishes regenerative agriculture research
* Trains staff and producers
* Navigates debates about regenerative claims and standards

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes regenerative agriculture science and policy
Application: Provides regenerative agriculture leadership
Indicators:
* Develops regenerative agriculture standards or frameworks
* Influences policy supporting regenerative transition
* Leads major regenerative agriculture initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises on regenerative agriculture strategy

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: NRCS climate-smart programs, working lands conservation, program standards
Private Sector: Regenerative supply chains, certification, corporate sustainability
NGOs: Regenerative agriculture advocacy, farmer networks, technical assistance
Consulting: Regenerative transition support, outcome assessment
Academia: Regenerative agriculture research, systems analysis

________________


1.8.4 Agricultural-ecological integration
Managing agricultural landscapes to optimize both production and ecological function, including habitat, water quality, and biodiversity.

Job Tasks:
1. Assess agricultural landscapes for ecological function and habitat value
2. Design farm or landscape configurations that support both production and conservation
3. Evaluate conservation practice effectiveness on working lands

Proficiency Levels & Assessment Indicators:

FOUNDATIONAL (Entry-level, 0-2 years)
Knowledge: Understands that farms can provide habitat and ecological services
Application: Recognizes conservation features in agricultural landscapes
Indicators:
* Identifies habitat features on farms (hedgerows, wetlands, field margins, ponds)
* Understands conservation practice purposes (filter strips, buffers, cover crops)
* Recognizes wildlife using agricultural habitats
* Understands concept of farmland biodiversity
* Identifies water quality connections to agricultural management

INTERMEDIATE (3-5 years)
Knowledge: Applies landscape ecology to agricultural systems
Application: Assesses and improves ecological function on working lands
Indicators:
* Evaluates habitat quality and connectivity in agricultural landscapes
* Recommends conservation practice placement for wildlife or water
* Assesses pollinator and beneficial insect habitat
* Integrates conservation with farm operations
* Uses landscape analysis to target conservation investment
* Communicates conservation value of working lands
* Documents ecological outcomes of conservation practices

ADVANCED (6-10 years)
Knowledge: Expert in agricultural-ecological integration across scales
Application: Leads working lands conservation programs
Indicators:
* Designs landscape-scale conservation strategies for agricultural regions
* Evaluates conservation program effectiveness
* Develops targeting and prioritization frameworks
* Integrates multiple conservation objectives (water, habitat, carbon, production)
* Publishes working lands conservation research
* Trains staff in agricultural-ecological integration
* Develops conservation practice standards and guidance

EXPERT (10+ years)
Knowledge: Shapes working lands conservation practice and policy
Application: Provides leadership on agricultural conservation
Indicators:
* Develops conservation policy for working lands
* Influences Farm Bill and agricultural conservation programs
* Leads major working lands conservation initiatives
* Publishes influential research
* Advises on agricultural conservation strategy

Sector-Specific Applications:
Public Agencies: NRCS conservation planning, CRP/EQIP, state working lands programs
Private Sector: Sustainable sourcing, habitat credits, biodiversity assessment
NGOs: Working lands conservation, farmland bird conservation, watershed programs
Consulting: Conservation planning, habitat assessment, water quality
