DOMAIN-LEVEL COMPETENCY FRAMEWORK
________________


CROSS-CUTTING COMPETENCIES FOR POLICY & REGULATORY SKILLS
Beyond technical skills in specific areas, effective policy and regulatory professionals demonstrate competencies that span across all skill areas in this domain:
1. Systems Thinking and Integration
Description: The ability to understand complex policy systems with multiple interacting components, recognizing how legal frameworks, economic mechanisms, social equity concerns, and environmental outcomes interconnect. This includes seeing beyond single-issue solutions to understand ripple effects, unintended consequences, and opportunities for integrated approaches.
Why It Matters: Environmental challenges don't respect disciplinary or regulatory boundaries. Climate change intersects with environmental justice, economic development, land use planning, and public health. Effective policy professionals must integrate across skill areas—recognizing, for example, how carbon markets relate to environmental justice, or how land use planning affects air quality in overburdened communities. Systems thinking prevents siloed solutions that solve one problem while creating others.
Application Examples:
* Designing carbon market programs that explicitly address environmental justice concerns and ensure equitable distribution of benefits
* Developing land use plans that simultaneously advance climate adaptation, affordable housing, and environmental health
* Creating regulatory approaches that coordinate across air, water, and waste programs to address cumulative impacts
* Structuring climate finance that advances both mitigation and adaptation while supporting vulnerable communities
Development Pathway:
* Foundational: Recognize that policy issues have multiple dimensions and stakeholders
* Intermediate: Analyze interactions between different policy instruments and programs
* Advanced: Design integrated policy approaches addressing multiple objectives simultaneously
* Expert: Transform policy systems through innovative integration frameworks
________________


2. Stakeholder Engagement and Negotiation
Description: The capacity to engage diverse stakeholders authentically, build trust across differences, facilitate meaningful participation, and negotiate solutions that advance environmental goals while addressing legitimate concerns. This includes cultural competency, conflict resolution, and the ability to work with communities historically excluded from decision-making.
Why It Matters: Policy and regulatory work inherently involves multiple stakeholders with different values, interests, and power. Effective environmental outcomes require not just technically sound policies, but policies that have social legitimacy and political durability. This demands genuine engagement—particularly with environmental justice communities, tribal nations, and others whose voices have been marginalized. Negotiation skills enable finding pathways forward when interests conflict.
Application Examples:
* Facilitating government-to-government consultation with tribal nations that builds lasting partnerships
* Engaging environmental justice communities meaningfully in permitting decisions, ensuring their concerns shape outcomes
* Negotiating interagency agreements that align different organizational missions and authorities
* Building coalitions across diverse stakeholders for policy advocacy or collaborative conservation
* Mediating conflicts between development interests and conservation priorities to find mutually beneficial solutions
Development Pathway:
* Foundational: Listen respectfully to diverse perspectives and communicate clearly
* Intermediate: Facilitate stakeholder processes and negotiate agreement terms
* Advanced: Build enduring partnerships across stakeholders and resolve complex conflicts
* Expert: Transform stakeholder relationships and engagement practices systemically
________________


3. Adaptive Capacity and Learning Orientation
Description: The ability to work effectively amid uncertainty, adjust approaches based on new information, and continuously learn from implementation experience. This includes comfort with experimentation, willingness to acknowledge when approaches aren't working, and capacity to revise policies and practices based on evidence.
Why It Matters: The policy and regulatory landscape is constantly evolving—new environmental challenges emerge (forever chemicals, microplastics, novel ecosystems), markets and finance mechanisms innovate rapidly, climate impacts exceed predictions, and social expectations shift. Rigid adherence to established approaches becomes maladaptive. Effective professionals embrace learning, integrate new science and technology, and use adaptive management principles. This is especially critical for emerging areas like environmental markets, climate finance, and novel ecosystem management where best practices are still developing.
Application Examples:
* Designing carbon market programs with built-in evaluation and adjustment mechanisms
* Updating environmental justice screening tools as new research reveals cumulative impact pathways
* Revising land use plans based on monitoring of climate change impacts and adaptation effectiveness
* Adjusting regulatory approaches when implementation reveals unintended consequences or barriers
* Incorporating new science on emerging contaminants into risk assessment and standards
Development Pathway:
* Foundational: Recognize uncertainty and limits of current knowledge
* Intermediate: Monitor outcomes and adjust approaches based on feedback
* Advanced: Design adaptive policies with explicit learning mechanisms
* Expert: Lead organizational and field-wide learning and adaptation
________________


4. Legal and Economic Literacy
Description: Sufficient understanding of legal frameworks and economic principles to navigate regulatory systems, evaluate economic dimensions of policies, and integrate legal and economic considerations with environmental science and equity goals. This doesn't require being a lawyer or economist, but does require literacy in both domains.
Why It Matters: Environmental policy operates within legal constraints and economic realities. Professionals need to understand what's legally permissible, how to structure legally defensible regulations, how economic incentives drive behavior, and how to evaluate economic impacts and tradeoffs. This literacy enables effective communication with legal counsel and economists, realistic assessment of policy options, and integration of legal/economic considerations with other objectives rather than treating them as separate or opposed to environmental goals.
Application Examples:
* Designing market-based conservation mechanisms that are both economically efficient and legally enforceable
* Evaluating whether proposed regulations will withstand legal challenge
* Conducting regulatory impact analysis that properly accounts for benefits and costs
* Structuring conservation finance that meets legal requirements while achieving conservation outcomes
* Understanding treaty obligations and international law in transboundary conservation
Development Pathway:
* Foundational: Understand basic legal and economic concepts relevant to environmental management
* Intermediate: Apply legal and economic analysis to policy problems
* Advanced: Integrate legal, economic, and environmental considerations in sophisticated ways
* Expert: Shape how legal and economic tools serve environmental and equity goals
________________


5. Equity and Justice Orientation
Description: Consistent attention to how environmental policies, regulations, and programs distribute benefits and burdens across communities, with particular focus on historically marginalized and overburdened populations. This includes recognizing power dynamics, addressing procedural and substantive justice, and ensuring that environmental solutions don't perpetuate or worsen inequities.
Why It Matters: Environmental policy has often ignored or exacerbated environmental injustices—from siting of polluting facilities in communities of color to conservation approaches that displace indigenous peoples. Achieving just and durable environmental outcomes requires centering equity throughout policy development and implementation. This competency transforms from "add equity and stir" to fundamentally rethinking how policies are developed, who participates in decisions, and how outcomes are evaluated. It's essential across all skill areas in this domain—from environmental law to markets to land use planning.
Application Examples:
* Conducting environmental justice screening for all major policies and projects
* Ensuring carbon market programs direct benefits to frontline communities and don't create local pollution hotspots
* Designing land use planning processes that meaningfully include marginalized communities from initial visioning through implementation
* Evaluating regulatory alternatives for distributional impacts across income and racial groups
* Structuring climate finance that prioritizes adaptation needs of most vulnerable communities
* Addressing historical exclusion of tribal nations from environmental decision-making
Development Pathway:
* Foundational: Recognize environmental injustices and understand procedural/distributive justice concepts
* Intermediate: Integrate equity analysis into policy work and engage marginalized communities authentically
* Advanced: Design policies that advance environmental justice and center community priorities
* Expert: Transform policy systems to address systemic environmental injustice
________________


IMPLEMENTATION GUIDANCE FOR ORGANIZATIONS
For Public Agencies
Building Policy & Regulatory Capacity:
1. Invest in diverse skill development: While some staff may specialize deeply in one area (environmental law, economics, EJ), build organizational capacity across all skill areas. Cross-train staff to understand connections between areas.

2. Institutionalize equity and justice: Move beyond individual EJ coordinators to embed equity analysis throughout permitting, planning, rulemaking, and enforcement. Train all policy staff in EJ assessment and meaningful community engagement.

3. Develop adaptive capacity: Build monitoring and evaluation into policies and programs from the start. Create feedback loops that enable learning and adjustment. Reward innovation and thoughtful risk-taking, not just compliance with established procedures.

4. Strengthen intergovernmental coordination: Invest in relationships across jurisdictions and agencies. Participate actively in interagency forums. Develop formal agreements that clarify roles and enable collaboration.

5. Stay current with emerging areas: Provide professional development in rapidly evolving areas like environmental markets, climate finance, emerging contaminants, and novel ecosystem management. Encourage staff attendance at conferences and training.

6. Build technical tools and systems: Invest in GIS capacity, screening tools (EJSCREEN), carbon accounting systems, climate scenario planning tools, and data platforms that enable sophisticated analysis.

7. Support tribal coordination: Ensure staff understand tribal sovereignty, trust responsibilities, and consultation requirements. Build authentic government-to-government relationships. Create dedicated positions for tribal liaisons.

Organizational Structure Considerations:
   * Consider integrating environmental justice and equity specialists across program areas rather than siloing in separate offices
   * Create cross-program teams for issues spanning jurisdictions (climate, watersheds, cumulative impacts)
   * Develop career ladders that value both deep specialization and cross-cutting expertise
   * Build bridges between traditionally separate programs (air, water, land use, health)
________________


For Private Sector Organizations
Building Policy & Regulatory Capacity:
   1. Develop compliance expertise: Ensure staff understand applicable environmental laws and regulatory requirements thoroughly. Build systems ensuring ongoing compliance monitoring and adaptation to regulatory changes.

   2. Integrate environmental considerations early: Engage policy and regulatory staff in project planning from inception, not just when permits are needed. This enables identification of environmental opportunities and constraints early.

   3. Build relationships with regulators: Establish professional, transparent relationships with agencies. Participate constructively in regulatory processes. Build trust through consistent compliance and responsiveness.

   4. Invest in environmental markets and finance: Build capacity in carbon markets, biodiversity credits, and green finance. Understand how these mechanisms can support both business objectives and environmental outcomes.

   5. Address environmental justice proactively: Assess environmental justice implications of operations and projects. Engage affected communities meaningfully. Address legacy environmental justice issues.

   6. Develop scenario planning capabilities: Use climate scenarios and land use planning to evaluate long-term risks and opportunities. Integrate climate risk into financial planning.

   7. Participate in policy development: Engage constructively in rulemaking, standard-setting, and legislative processes. Provide technical expertise. Support policy approaches that advance environmental goals while enabling business operations.

Organizational Considerations:
      * Ensure environmental/regulatory staff have access to senior leadership and influence strategic decisions
      * Integrate sustainability/environmental goals into core business strategy, not just compliance or corporate social responsibility
      * Build cross-functional teams linking environmental staff with operations, finance, and planning
      * Consider obtaining third-party certifications (ISO 14001, B Corp, climate commitments) that create accountability
________________


For NGOs and Community Organizations
Building Policy & Regulatory Capacity:
      1. Develop policy advocacy expertise: Build staff capacity across policy research, analysis, drafting, and advocacy. Understand legislative and regulatory processes thoroughly.

      2. Center environmental justice: Ensure organizational leadership includes people from frontline communities. Use community-based participatory research approaches. Direct resources to community-led environmental justice organizations.

      3. Build legal capacity: Either develop in-house legal expertise or build strong partnerships with environmental law organizations. Understand when litigation is appropriate tool.

      4. Engage in market mechanisms strategically: Evaluate environmental markets critically—when do they advance conservation and when do they create problems? Participate in standard-setting to ensure integrity.

      5. Coordinate across organizations: Build coalitions for advocacy. Specialize where appropriate (some orgs focus on EJ, others on markets, others on climate finance) but coordinate strategies.

      6. Develop technical sophistication: Build capacity in economic analysis, climate finance, carbon accounting, and other technical areas to engage credibly with agencies and private sector.

      7. Support community capacity: Provide technical assistance to communities navigating regulatory processes, accessing climate finance, or evaluating proposed projects.

Organizational Considerations:
         * Ensure diversity in staff and leadership, particularly including people from environmental justice communities
         * Balance insider access and outsider advocacy—both have roles
         * Invest in long-term relationship-building with communities, not just project-based engagement
         * Share power and resources with grassroots organizations
________________


For Consulting Firms
Building Policy & Regulatory Capacity:
         1. Maintain current regulatory expertise: Stay abreast of changing regulations, guidance, and court decisions. Subscribe to regulatory tracking services. Participate in professional organizations.

         2. Build multidisciplinary teams: Integrate policy, legal, economic, ecological, and social expertise. Avoid disciplinary silos. Encourage cross-training.

         3. Develop environmental justice expertise: Build authentic relationships with EJ communities. Understand power dynamics in consulting relationships. Ensure community benefit from projects.

         4. Invest in specialized areas: Develop deep expertise in emerging areas like environmental markets, climate finance, tribal consultation, or novel ecosystem management where demand is growing.

         5. Maintain integrity in advisory roles: Provide honest, technically sound advice even when it's not what clients want to hear. Support environmental protection while serving clients.

         6. Stay current with technology: Invest in GIS, modeling tools, screening tools, and databases. Use technology to increase analysis quality and efficiency.

         7. Contribute to practice advancement: Publish case studies, develop methods, present at conferences. Give back to field.

Business Considerations:
            * Develop services in growth areas (EJ assessment, climate finance, carbon markets, adaptation planning)
            * Build repeat client relationships through quality work and responsiveness
            * Consider B Corp or similar certification to formalize environmental/social commitments
            * Invest in staff professional development
________________


For Academic Institutions
Building Policy & Regulatory Capacity:
            1. Integrate across disciplines: Environmental policy programs should integrate law, economics, natural science, public health, and social science. Break down disciplinary silos.

            2. Emphasize applied skills: Beyond theory, ensure students develop practical skills in policy analysis, stakeholder engagement, GIS, economic analysis, and regulatory navigation.

            3. Center environmental justice: Integrate EJ throughout curriculum, not just in elective courses. Include EJ community voices as instructors and partners. Support students from underrepresented communities.

            4. Partner with practice: Develop practicum courses placing students with agencies, NGOs, tribes, and communities. Bring practitioners as instructors. Conduct research addressing real-world policy questions.

            5. Stay current with emerging issues: Rapidly incorporate new topics (climate finance, biodiversity credits, PFAS, novel ecosystems) into curriculum as they emerge.

            6. Teach adaptive capacity: Help students develop comfort with uncertainty, systems thinking, and learning orientation—not just mastery of current best practices.

            7. Develop ethical frameworks: Help students wrestle with value conflicts, power dynamics, and ethical dilemmas inherent in environmental policy work.

Curricular Considerations:
               * Ensure environmental policy programs include environmental law, economics, justice, and science
               * Require practical experience through internships, practica, or capstone projects
               * Teach both technical skills (GIS, modeling, carbon accounting) and soft skills (facilitation, negotiation, communication)
               * Include tribal perspectives and international dimensions
________________


IMPLEMENTATION GUIDANCE FOR INDIVIDUALS
Building Your Policy & Regulatory Skills
For Early Career Professionals:
               1. Build foundational breadth: Develop literacy across multiple skill areas even as you begin specializing. Understand how environmental law, economics, land use planning, and environmental justice interconnect.

               2. Seek diverse experiences: Early in your career, work across different contexts (agency, NGO, consulting) if possible. Experience different roles (analyst, field staff, engagement coordinator) to understand the system from multiple angles.

               3. Develop technical skills: Build competency in tools that span skill areas: GIS, statistical analysis, economic modeling, regulatory databases. These technical skills combined with policy knowledge make you highly valuable.

               4. Learn from mentors: Seek out experienced policy professionals who can help you navigate complexity, understand informal systems, and develop judgment.

               5. Practice communication: Policy work requires translating technical information for diverse audiences—elected officials, communities, media, scientists. Practice writing clearly and speaking accessibly.

               6. Understand power and equity: Learn to recognize power dynamics in policy processes. Develop cultural humility. Build authentic relationships with communities different from your own.

               7. Stay curious: The policy landscape constantly evolves. Cultivate learning orientation. Read widely. Attend webinars and conferences. Join professional organizations.

Recommended Early Career Experiences:
                  * Regulatory compliance role to understand how regulations actually work in practice
                  * Community engagement or environmental justice position to learn authentic engagement
                  * Legislative or policy analysis position to understand policy development processes
                  * Tribal liaison or coordination role to learn government-to-government relationships
________________


For Mid-Career Professionals:
Deepening and Expanding Expertise:
                  1. Develop specialized expertise: By mid-career, develop deep expertise in 2-3 skill areas while maintaining breadth. Become known for specific capabilities (carbon markets, environmental justice, NEPA, tribal consultation, climate finance).

                  2. Build strategic skills: Move beyond technical proficiency to strategic thinking. Understand political context, timing, coalition-building, and how change actually happens.

                  3. Lead initiatives: Take on leadership of projects, teams, or programs. Develop skills in project management, team leadership, and organizational navigation.

                  4. Span boundaries: Mid-career professionals are often most effective at bridging between specialties, sectors, or organizations. Cultivate this integrative capacity.

                  5. Build professional network: Develop relationships across sectors and organizations. These networks enable collaboration, information-sharing, and career opportunities.

                  6. Mentor others: Share knowledge with early-career professionals. This both helps the field and deepens your own expertise.

                  7. Contribute to practice: Present at conferences, write articles or case studies, serve on working groups or committees. Help advance the field beyond your organization.

Professional Development Focus:
                     * Advanced training in specialized areas (environmental law, carbon accounting, climate finance)
                     * Leadership and management development
                     * Strategic planning and systems change
                     * Facilitation and negotiation skills
                     * Grant writing and fundraising (especially for NGO careers)
________________


For Senior Professionals:
Leading and Transforming Practice:
                     1. Provide strategic leadership: Shape organizational or field-wide direction. Influence policy priorities and approaches. Think systemically about transformative change.

                     2. Build and leverage influence: Use expertise, relationships, and reputation to advance policy goals. Know when to work inside systems and when to challenge from outside.

                     3. Develop others: Invest significantly in mentoring, teaching, and capacity building. Your career success increasingly depends on developing others.

                     4. Integrate across silos: Use senior position to break down organizational or field-wide silos. Create connections between people and topics that don't naturally interact.

                     5. Take principled risks: Use career capital to advocate for approaches that may be controversial but needed—centering environmental justice, confronting systemic racism, challenging inadequate policies.

                     6. Contribute to policy transformation: Don't just work within existing policy paradigms—help shift paradigms. Question assumptions. Develop innovative approaches.

                     7. Stay current: Even as a senior professional, continue learning. New issues (microplastics, forever chemicals, novel ecosystems, new financial mechanisms) continue emerging.

Leadership Considerations:
                        * Balance maintaining technical expertise with developing strategic and political skills
                        * Recognize when to step aside and amplify others' voices, especially from marginalized communities
                        * Use privilege and platform to advance equity and justice
                        * Think about legacy—what will be your lasting contribution to the field?
________________


Continuing Education and Professional Development
Professional Organizations and Networks:
                        * Environmental Law Institute (ELI)
                        * National Association of Environmental Professionals (NAEP)
                        * Association of Environmental and Resource Economists (AERE)
                        * Environmental Justice organizations and networks
                        * Sector-specific associations (state associations of environmental professionals, tribal environmental organizations)
                        * Climate finance and carbon market networks
Recommended Certifications and Credentials:
                        * Juris Doctor (J.D.) for environmental law careers
                        * Ph.D. in Economics, Policy, or related field for senior research or academic roles
                        * Professional certifications (CHMM, CPESC, CPSS, AICP) depending on specialization
                        * Specialized trainings (GHG certification, EJ certificate programs, climate finance training)
Key Conferences and Forums:
                        * National Association of Environmental Professionals conference
                        * Environmental Justice conferences and summits
                        * Climate finance and carbon market conferences
                        * Tribal lands and environment forum
                        * Land use planning conferences (APA, urban planning associations)
________________


ACKNOWLEDGMENTS & USAGE NOTES
About This Taxonomy
This skills taxonomy for environmental management workforce was developed to serve multiple purposes:
                        * Workforce Development: Helping organizations identify skill needs and develop training programs
                        * Hiring and Recruitment: Providing clear skill descriptions and proficiency expectations
                        * Curriculum Design: Guiding academic programs in environmental policy, planning, and management
                        * Career Planning: Helping individuals chart career pathways and identify development areas
                        * Performance Management: Supporting clear performance expectations and evaluation
How to Use This Resource
For Employers:
                        * Use skill descriptions and proficiency levels to develop position descriptions
                        * Assess organizational capacity gaps and training needs
                        * Design professional development programs aligned with proficiency level progressions
                        * Create competency-based evaluation systems
For Educators:
                        * Align curriculum with skill areas and proficiency expectations
                        * Ensure programs address both technical skills and cross-cutting competencies
                        * Use job role profiles to understand employer expectations
                        * Design student assessments around proficiency indicators
For Job Seekers:
                        * Identify skill areas matching your interests and strengths
                        * Assess your current proficiency levels honestly
                        * Target development in areas important for desired roles
                        * Use terminology in applications and interviews
For Career Planning:
                        * Explore different career pathways to understand options
                        * Identify skills needed for advancement
                        * Plan professional development strategically
                        * Understand how to progress from one proficiency level to the next
Important Principles
Accessibility: This taxonomy is written in clear language accessible to diverse audiences, avoiding jargon where possible and explaining technical terms when necessary.
Cross-Sector Applicability: Content applies across public agencies, private sector, NGOs, consulting, and academia. Sector-specific applications are noted but the core skills transcend sectors.
No Credential Prescription: Credential examples are provided for context but should not be interpreted as requirements. Many paths lead to competency, and credentials are only one form of qualification.
Progressive Complexity: Proficiency levels build logically from foundational to expert, describing increasing independence, complexity, and scope of influence.
Observable Indicators: Proficiency indicators focus on observable behaviors and capabilities that can be demonstrated and assessed.
Equity and Justice: This taxonomy centers environmental justice and equity throughout, reflecting that these considerations must be integrated into all environmental policy work, not treated as separate or optional.
Living Document: Environmental policy and regulatory practice continues evolving. This taxonomy represents current understanding but should be updated periodically to reflect emerging issues, new technologies, and evolving best practices.
Limitations and Considerations
                        * Individual Variation: Actual career progressions vary. Some may develop expertise faster or slower than typical timeframes suggest. Non-traditional pathways are valid.

                        * Context Matters: Proficiency requirements vary by organization size, complexity, and context. A "mid-level" position in one organization might require advanced skills in another.

                        * Specialization vs. Breadth: This taxonomy presents skills separately for clarity, but in practice, professionals blend skills across areas. The most effective professionals integrate multiple skill areas.

                        * Emerging Areas: Some skill areas (environmental markets, climate finance, environmental justice) are rapidly evolving. Best practices continue developing.

                        * Geographic Variation: Specific regulations, programs, and standards vary by jurisdiction. This taxonomy focuses on transferable principles.

Feedback and Updates
This taxonomy will be periodically reviewed and updated to remain current with evolving practice. Feedback from practitioners, educators, and employers helps improve the resource. Suggested additions or revisions are welcome.
________________


CONCLUSION
Domain 6: Policy & Regulatory Skills encompasses the legal, economic, and institutional frameworks essential for environmental management. From environmental law to land use planning, resource economics to environmental markets, intergovernmental relations to environmental justice, climate finance to air quality management—these skills enable professionals to navigate complex regulatory systems, shape effective policies, ensure equitable outcomes, and leverage market mechanisms for environmental benefit.
As environmental challenges grow more complex and interconnected, the need for sophisticated policy and regulatory expertise increases. Climate change, environmental justice, novel ecosystems, emerging contaminants, and rapidly evolving financial mechanisms demand professionals who can integrate across traditional boundaries, work respectfully with diverse stakeholders, adapt to uncertainty, and center equity in all aspects of policy work.
The professionals who excel in this domain combine technical competency with systems thinking, stakeholder engagement skills with legal and economic literacy, and specialized expertise with cross-cutting integration. They work across sectors and jurisdictions, building bridges between science and policy, environment and economy, regulation and innovation, historical injustice and future equity.
Whether you are developing regulatory compliance programs, structuring carbon market projects, facilitating tribal consultation, designing land use plans, assessing environmental health risks, or mobilizing climate finance—the skills in this domain enable you to translate environmental goals into actionable policy, ensure legal and economic feasibility, engage stakeholders authentically, and advance environmental justice. These are the skills that determine not just what environmental outcomes are possible, but how we achieve them and who benefits.
________________


This completes Domain 6: Policy & Regulatory Skills. For comprehensive workforce development, this domain should be integrated with the other five domains of the environmental management skills taxonomy, recognizing that effective environmental professionals often blend skills across multiple domains.
